## Where it started
Joshua compacted mid-run and said to continue with batch 09, handle all of it, and make sure everything claimed as verified is actually true. His standing instructions from earlier in the run still apply:
- Write, load and verify about 40 explanations per batch with guarded insert-only SQL against Lovable Cloud project `53b9a6f2-aba7-4ae4-8dd1-fa5b8aab4d76`.

- The free `deploy_project` is pre-authorized. Push only your own commits.

## Decisions locked + what shipped
- **Batch 09 loaded: 40 Principle slots.** These were every gap labelled Principle-Conform, "Principle Conform", "Principle" or Principle-PSA.
  - Breakdown: 23 unexplained, 12 unloaded (question and choices inserted from the bank), 2 partial, 2 notes-only, 1 steps-only.
  - Drafts: `C:\Users\Joshu\dev\ai-os\corpus\lr-explanations\drafts\batch-09-principle-part-a.md` (23 slots) and `C:\Users\Joshu\dev\ai-os\corpus\lr-explanations\drafts\batch-09-principle-part-b.md` (17 slots). Both are marked `status: loaded 2026-10-02`.
- **Partial and notes-only slots got only what was missing:**
  - PT108-S3-Q16 got its C note. PT119-S2-Q15 got its E note.
  - PT118-S1-Q9 and PT118-S3-Q8 got five notes each on their `standard` explanation rows. Those rows had steps but no notes; their notes live on the `another_way` rows.
  - PT143-S1-Q2 got steps only.
- **Eight unloaded slots retyped to Principle-Strengthen,** because the stem asks for a justifying principle: PT132-S4-Q20, PT143-S3-Q3, PT144-S2-Q6, PT145-S4-Q1, PT156-S2-Q12, PT156-S4-Q26, PT159-S1-Q14, PT159-S3-Q1.
  - PT128-S2-Q14's header was corrected from "Principle Conform" to "Principle-Conform" before loading. SQL was regenerated and only chunk 8 changed.
- **Step-label convention used:**
  - Rule / Evaluate / Goal when the rule is in the stimulus.
  - Situation / Evaluate / Goal when the answers generalize a situation.
  - Conclusion / Evidence / Evaluate / Goal for arguments and justify questions.
- **Writing rules held:** no gendered pronouns in explanation text (names used throughout), and no em dashes.
- **Bank fix: PR vnjkoxpl-42905/lsatu-684d89db#190, merged as `124fee3`.**
  - PT119-S2-Q15 choice E: the U+FFFD character in "jalape�o" was restored to "ñ".

from pathlib import Path
from collections import defaultdict

DB = R / "system/scripts/lsatlab-pull/data/db"

L = lambda n: json.loads((DB / f"{n}.json").read_text(encoding="utf-8"))
snap = json.loads((R / "raw/sources/lsatu-lr-snapshots/2026-10-02-pre-batch09-load.json").read_text(encoding="utf-8"))
man = json.loads((W / "batch09-sql-manifest.json").read_text(encoding="utf-8"))
Q = L("lr_questions"); E = L("lr_explanations"); S = L("lr_explanation_steps"); N = L("lr_explanation_choice_notes"); C = L("lr_answer_choices")
qby = {q["qid"]: q for q in Q}
exq = defaultdict(list)
for e in E: exq[e["question_id"]].append(e)
st = defaultdict(list)
for s in S: st[s["explanation_id"]].append(s)
nt = defaultdict(list)
for n in N: nt[n["explanation_id"]].append(n)
DQ = re.compile(r"\$batch09\$(.*?)\$batch09\$", re.S)
fails = []
for m in man:
    slot = m["slot"]; sql = (W / m["file"]).read_text(encoding="utf-8")
    q = qby[slot]
    exps = [e for e in exq[q["id"]] if e["style"] in ("single", "standard")]
    if len(exps) != 1: fails.append(f"{slot}: {len(exps)} single/standard rows"); continue
    e = exps[0]
    steps = sorted(st[e["id"]], key=lambda s: s["position"])
    notes = {n["letter"]: n["body"] for n in nt[e["id"]]}
    for line in sql.splitlines():
        if line.startswith("INSERT INTO lr_explanation_steps"):
            vals = DQ.findall(line.split("CROSS JOIN (VALUES", 1)[1].split(") v(", 1)[0])
            exp = list(zip(vals[0::2], vals[1::2]))
            got = [(s["label"], s["body"]) for s in steps]
            if got != exp: fails.append(f"{slot}: steps differ")
        if line.startswith("INSERT INTO lr_explanation_choice_notes"):
            vals = DQ.findall(line.split("CROSS JOIN (VALUES", 1)[1].split(") v(", 1)[0])
            exp = dict(zip(vals[0::2], vals[1::2]))
            if {k: notes.get(k) for k in exp} != exp: fails.append(f"{slot}: inserted notes differ")
            old = {n["letter"]: n["body"] for n in snap["lr_explanation_choice_notes"] if n["explanation_id"] == e["id"]}
            if set(old) & set(exp): fails.append(f"{slot}: inserted over existing letter")
            if {k: notes.get(k) for k in old} != old: fails.append(f"{slot}: pre-existing notes changed")
            if set(notes) != set(old) | set(exp): fails.append(f"{slot}: unexpected extra notes")
    if [s["position"] for s in steps] != list(range(len(steps))): fails.append(f"{slot}: positions not contiguous")
    if sorted(notes) != list("ABCDE"): fails.append(f"{slot}: notes not A-E")
    if m["state"] in ("partial","notes-only"):
        before = sorted([(s["id"], s["position"], s["label"], s["body"]) for s in snap["lr_explanation_steps"] if s["explanation_id"] == e["id"]])
        after = sorted([(s["id"], s["position"], s["label"], s["body"]) for s in steps])
        if before != after: fails.append(f"{slot}: pre-existing steps changed")
# questions, choices, answer keys untouched for the 40 targets
for q in snap["lr_questions"]:
    now = qby[q["qid"]]
    for k in ("stimulus", "prompt", "correct_choice"):
        if now[k] != q[k]: fails.append(f"{q['qid']}: question {k} changed")
cnow = {c["id"]: c for c in C}
for c in snap["lr_answer_choices"]:
    n = cnow.get(c["id"])

"""Validate a batch of LR explanation drafts (usage: generate_batch_sql.py NN [bank_dir]) and emit guarded insert-only SQL, one transaction per slot. No database writes.

Handles four slot states:
  unloaded     question absent: insert question + choices (bank text) + explanation
  unexplained  question present, no single/standard explanation: insert explanation
  notes-only   explanation has steps but no notes: insert five notes
  partial      explanation has steps and some notes: insert only the missing letters
"""

import sys
from pathlib import Path

DRAFTS = sorted((ROOT / "corpus/lr-explanations/drafts").glob(f"batch-{BATCH}-*-part-*.md"))
DB = ROOT / "system/scripts/lsatlab-pull/data/db"
BANK = Path(sys.argv[2]) if len(sys.argv) > 2 else project_path("lsatu-wt-bankfix-b02", "public", "data")
OUT = ROOT / f"corpus/lr-explanations/work/batch-{BATCH}-inputs"

TEXT_FIX = {("PT127-S1-Q4", "questionStem"): ("�", "’")}


def lit(s):
    if Q in s:
        raise ValueError("delimiter in text")
    return f"{Q}{s}{Q}"


def norm_text(s):
    """Collapse hard line wraps inside paragraphs; keep blank-line paragraph breaks."""
    s = re.sub(r"(?<=[A-Za-z0-9])-[ \t]*\n\s*(?=[a-z])", "-", s)  # compound split at a hard wrap
    paras = re.split(r"\n\s*\n", s.strip())
    return "\n\n".join(re.sub(r"\s*\n\s*", " ", p).strip() for p in paras)


SPEAKER = r"(?:(?:Mr|Ms|Mrs|Dr)\. )?[A-Z][A-Za-z]+(?: [A-Za-z]+)?: "


def speaker_breaks(s):
    """In a dialogue stimulus, start each later speaker on its own line (matches the database convention)."""
    if not re.match(SPEAKER, s):
        return s
    def brk(m):
        return m.group(1) + (" " if m.group(1) in ("Mr.", "Ms.", "Mrs.", "Dr.") else "\n")
    return re.sub(r"(\S*[.?!”\"])\s+(?=" + SPEAKER + ")", brk, s)


def records(name):
    return json.loads((DB / f"{name}.json").read_text(encoding="utf-8"))


def parse_drafts():

# Batch 09, part A: Principle questions, PT102 to PT120

Twenty-three of the 40 Principle gaps. Most are in the database with no explanation. PT108-S3-Q16 is missing only its C note and PT119-S2-Q15 only its E note. PT118-S1-Q9 and PT118-S3-Q8 have steps on the standard explanation but no notes, so they get five notes each and no new steps.

Modelled on `models/Principle.md`: put the judgment on the right side of the rule, check the outcome first and the trigger second, and give the most-picked trap the fullest note.

Labels: a rule in the stimulus that the answers apply gets Rule / Evaluate / Goal. A situation that the answers generalize gets Situation / Evaluate / Goal. An argument that a principle must fit or justify gets Conclusion / Evidence / Evaluate / Goal.

## PT102-S2-Q11 | Principle-Conform | D

> A Habitat Conservation Plan (HCP) is based on a law that allows developers to use land inhabited by endangered species in exchange for a promise to preserve critical habitat or provide replacement land nearby. Some individuals of endangered species are lost in return for assurances by the owner or developer that habitat for those remaining animals will be protected. Environmentalists are pleased that HCPs allow them to win concessions from developers who would otherwise ignore rarely enforced environmental laws. Satisfied property owners prefer HCPs to more restrictive prohibitions of land use.
>
> The situation described above most closely conforms to which one of the following principles?
>
> (A) In order to avoid protracted legal battles environmentalists should compromise with developers.
> (B) Developers should adhere only to those environmental laws that are not overburdensome.
> (C) Laws should not be designed to serve the interests of all the parties concerned since they are often so weak that no one's interest is served well.
> (D) Laws should be fashioned in such a way as to reconcile the interests of developers and environmentalists.
> (E) The most effective means of preserving endangered species is to refrain from alienating property owners.

### Argument Breakdown

**Situation:** Under an HCP, developers get to use land where endangered species live. In return, they protect critical habitat or provide replacement land. Environmentalists like it because they win concessions from developers who would otherwise ignore weak laws. Property owners like it because it beats stricter bans.

**Evaluate:** The last two sentences are the payoff: both sides are satisfied. Each gives something up and each gets something it wants. A law that leaves opposing parties both pleased is a law built to balance their interests.

**Goal:** A principle about laws that reconcile the interests of developers and environmentalists.

### Choice Notes

**(A) Not Described.** Nobody mentions legal battles. The compromise here comes from the design of a law, not from environmentalists trying to stay out of court.

**(B) Not Described.** Developers here give up land and make promises. Nothing says they get to skip laws they find burdensome.

**(C) Opposite.** The HCP serves the interests of both parties, and both are satisfied. This principle says laws shouldn't try to do that.

**(D) Correct.** Developers get to build, environmentalists get habitat protection, and both are pleased. That is a law fashioned to reconcile their interests.

**(E) Too Strong.** "The most effective means" ranks every option for saving species. The passage describes one arrangement that pleases everyone. It never compares it to anything else or says species fare best this way.

## PT102-S4-Q14 | Principle Conform | D

> We have a moral obligation not to destroy books, even if they belong to us. The reason is quite simple: If preserved, books will almost certainly contribute to the intellectual and emotional enrichment of future generations.
>
> Which one of the following most accurately expresses the principle underlying the argument?
>
> (A) It is morally incumbent upon us to devote effort to performing actions that have at least some chance of improving other people’s lives.
> (B) We are morally obligated to preserve anything that past generations had preserved for our intellectual and emotional enrichment.
> (C) The moral commitments we have to future generations supersede the moral commitments we have to the present generation.
> (D) We are morally obligated not to destroy anything that will most likely enrich, either intellectually or emotionally, our posterity.
> (E) Being morally obligated not to destroy something requires that we be reasonably assured that that thing will lead to the betterment of someone we know.

### Argument Breakdown

**Conclusion:** We have a moral obligation not to destroy books, even our own.

**Evidence:** If preserved, books will almost certainly enrich future generations, intellectually and emotionally.

**Evaluate:** The argument moves from "this thing will very likely enrich people who come after us" to "we're obligated not to destroy it." The principle has to have that fact on the left and the obligation not to destroy on the right: if something will very likely enrich posterity, we must not destroy it.

**Goal:** If a thing will most likely enrich future generations, we are morally obligated not to destroy it.

188	===== PT122-S1-Q17 Principle Strengthen KEY C
189	Sharon, a noted collector of fine glass, found a rare glass vase in a secondhand store in a small town she was visiting. The vase was priced at $10, but Sharon knew that it was worth at least $1,000. Saying nothing to the storekeeper about the value of the vase, Sharon bought the vase for $10. Weeks later the storekeeper read a newspaper article about Sharon's collection, which mentioned the vase and how she had acquired it. When the irate storekeeper later accused Sharon of taking advantage of him, Sharon replied that she had done nothing wrong.
190	Q: Which one of the following principles, if established, most helps to justify Sharon's position?
191	  A A seller is not obligated to inform a buyer of anything about the merchandise that the seller offers for sale except for the demanded price.
192	  B It is the responsibility of the seller, not the buyer, to make sure that the amount of money a buyer gives a seller in exchange for merchandise matches the amount that the seller demands for that merchandise.
193	  C A buyer's sole obligation to a seller is to pay in full the price that the seller demands for a piece of merchandise that the buyer acquires from the seller.
194	  D It is the responsibility of the buyer, not the seller, to ascertain that the quality of a piece of merchandise satisfies the buyer's standards.
195	  E The obligations that follow from any social relationship between two people who are well acquainted override any obligations that follow from an economic relationship between the two.
196	  DBtag A Bad Conclusion Match 2
197	  DBtag B Bad Evidence Match 15
198	  DBtag C Correct 82
199	  DBtag D Bad Conclusion Match / Out of Scope: quality 1
200	  DBtag E Out of Scope: well acquainted 0
201	  NOTE A | We need an answer that helps us conclude the buyer wasn't obligated to inform the seller of the real value of the merchandise. This answer is about the seller not being obligated to inform the buyer of something.
202	  NOTE B | This answer shifts responsibility from the buyer to the seller, which lines up with our conclusion that the buyer isn't responsible for telling the seller how much his goods are worth. But this answer does not line up with the facts of the case. The seller charged $10 and the buyer paid $10. The dispute isn't about the amount paid not matching the amount charged. The dispute is about the amount paid not matching the actual value of the merchandise.
203	  NOTE C | If the buyer's only obligation is to pay what the seller asks, which Sharon did, that absolves her of any wrongdoing that the seller is accusing her of. This answer is a little tricky because it doesn't use the same language as the judgement in the conclusion: it doesn't talk about right or wrong. But, if we have only one responsibility and we fulfill that responsibility, that implies that we held up our end of the bargain and didn't wrong the other party in the transaction.
204	  NOTE D | We need a principle that supports the judgment that the buyer did nothing wrong, This answer shifts responsibility from the seller to the buyer, which is the opposite of what we'd expect a right answer to do. It also focuses on ascertaining the quality of the merchandise, which is an out of scope idea.
205	  NOTE E | This principle is limited to people who are well acquainted. We don't have any evidence that Sharon and the seller are well acquainted, so this principle doesn't apply to them.

252	While playing a game with a ball, both Emma and John carelessly ignored the danger their game posed to nearby objects. An errant throw by John struck and broke a neighbor’s window. 253	Q: Which one of the following conforms most closely to the principle illustrated above?
254	  A While looking after her neighbor’s pets, Laura left the door to her neighbor’s house unlocked. Fortunately, nothing bad happened as a result. But her neighbor should not trust Laura to look after her pets in the future.
255	  B Gerald hired Linda and Seung to move his furniture to a new residence. Linda and Seung carefully followed Gerald’s instructions, but not all of the furniture fit in the moving truck. Gerald should still be required to pay Linda and Seung for the work they did.
256	  C Terry and Chris were racing their cars on a public street. Chris lost control of his car and struck a parked car. Chris, but not Terry, should be required to pay to repair the damage.
257	  D Alexis and Juan rented a boat for the afternoon. Because of improper use by the previous renter, the boat’s engine malfunctioned during their excursion. The boat’s owner should be required to refund Alexis’s and Juan’s rental fees.
258	  E Susan and Leland disregarded posted warnings in order to skate on a frozen pond. When the ice broke, Susan’s ankle was injured. Susan cannot hold the pond’s owners responsible for her injuries.
259	===== PT154-S2-Q4 Principle Strengthen KEY D
260	Editorial: Some primary schools’ early education reading programs encourage children to read by paying them for each book they read outside of class. Such programs should be viewed with suspicion. While paying kids to read might get them to read more, it also might teach them to regard reading as a chore rather than as a source of intrinsic satisfaction.
261	Q: Which one of the following principles, if valid, most strongly supports the editorial’s reasoning?
262	  A Early education reading programs should focus more on getting children to read challenging books than on getting them to read a large number of books.
263	  B Children will be more likely to develop into regular readers if they choose the books they read than if they do not.
264	  C Parents will usually play a more important role than teachers in instilling in children a love of reading.
265	  D The goal of early education reading programs should be to instill in children a love of reading.
266	  E Improving children’s facility with reading will get them to enjoy reading.
267	  DBtag A Out of Scope 1
268	  DBtag B Out of Scope 2
269	  DBtag C Irrelevant Comparison 0
270	  DBtag D Correct 94
271	  DBtag E Opposite (if anything) 3
272	  NOTE A | Encouraging children to read challenging books does not play any role in this particular argument. We might think that a book being challenging has something to do with children viewing reading as a chore, but the argument never states that. This answer might sound like it somehow supports the conclusion, but it doesn't strengthen the link between the evidence and the conclusion.
273	  NOTE B | Having children choose the books that they read does not play any role in this particular argument. The evidence discusses children reading more books because they're being paid to do it. What happens when children choose their own books is not relevant. This answer might sound like it somehow supports the conclusion, but it doesn't strengthen the link between the evidence and the conclusion.
274	  NOTE C | The argument never discusses exactly how parents or teachers are involved in the early reading programs. A comparison between the role of parents and the role teachers is irrelevant.
275	  NOTE D | This is one of those cases where the LSAT rewards us for reading very literally. The evidence discusses the possibility that kids will regard reading as a chore rather than as a source of intrinsic satisfaction. But the evidence presents this in a very factual way, and never actually states whether this is a good thing or a bad thing.
276	
277	We can strengthen the argument by stating that these reading programs should instill a love of reading in children. If they encourage children to view reading as an unsatisfying chore then the programs aren't achieving this goal, which is a reason to view them with suspicion.
278	  NOTE E | The evidence states that encouraging kids to read might cause them to view reading as a chore, not as something they intrinsically enjoy. This answer choice indicates that helping kids read will have the opposite result.
279	
280	We should also note that improving someone's facility with reading means to make reading easier. Making reading easier is not exactly the same as encouraging children to read. It's possible to encourage someone to do something even if the person finds it difficult.

            bd = sec.split("### Argument Breakdown", 1)

                for m in re.finditer(r"^\*\*(.+?):\*\* (.+?)(?=\n\n\*\*|\Z)", part.strip(), re.S | re.M):
                    steps.append((m.group(1).strip(), m.group(2).strip()))
            cn = sec.split("### Choice Notes", 1)[1] if "### Choice Notes" in sec else ""
            for m in re.finditer(r"^\*\*\(([A-E])\) (.+?)\.\*\* (.+?)(?=\n\n\*\*\(|\Z)", cn.strip(), re.S | re.M):

def main():
    drafts = parse_drafts()
    bank = {}
    for f in BANK.glob("PT*-S*-LR.json"):
        for b in json.loads(f.read_text(encoding="utf-8")):
            bank[f"{b['practiceTest']}-S{b['sectionNumber']}-Q{b['questionNumber']}"] = b
    qs = {q["qid"]: q for q in records("lr_questions")}
    exq = defaultdict(list)
    for e in records("lr_explanations"):
        if e["style"] in ("single", "standard"):
            exq[e["question_id"]].append(e)
    st = defaultdict(list)
    for s in records("lr_explanation_steps"):
        st[s["explanation_id"]].append(s)
    nt = defaultdict(dict)
    for n in records("lr_explanation_choice_notes"):
        nt[n["explanation_id"]][n["letter"]] = n["body"]

    errors, manifest = [], []
    OUT.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
    for slot, d in drafts.items():
        b = bank[slot]
        if d["key"] != b["correctAnswer"]:
            errors.append(f"{slot}: key mismatch")
        q = qs.get(slot)
        sql = [f"-- {slot}. Batch {BATCH}.", "BEGIN;"]
        if q is None:
            state = "unloaded"
            if len(d["steps"]) < 3 or sorted(d["notes"]) != list("ABCDE"):
                errors.append(f"{slot}: unloaded slot needs steps and five notes")
            pt, sec, num = int(b["practiceTest"][2:]), b["sectionNumber"], b["questionNumber"]
            stim = speaker_breaks(norm_text(b["stimulus"]))
            stem = norm_text(b["questionStem"])
            fix = TEXT_FIX.get((slot, "questionStem"))
            if fix:
                stem = stem.replace(*fix)
            if "�" in stim + stem + "".join(b["answerChoices"].values()):
                errors.append(f"{slot}: damaged character in bank text")
            qtype = TYPE_FIX.get(slot, d["type"])
            sql.append(f"DO $guard$ BEGIN IF EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM lr_questions q WHERE q.qid={lit(slot)}) THEN RAISE EXCEPTION 'Unloaded slot now exists: {slot}'; END IF; END $guard$;")
            sql.append(f"DO $guard$ BEGIN IF (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM lr_sections s WHERE s.preptest={pt} AND s.section={sec}) <> 1 THEN RAISE EXCEPTION 'Section unavailable: {slot}'; END IF; END $guard$;")
            sql.append(f"INSERT INTO lr_questions (section_id, qid, preptest, section, question_number, question_type, stimulus, prompt, correct_choice, is_explained, source_note) SELECT s.id, {lit(slot)}, {pt}, {sec}, {num}, {lit(qtype)}, {lit(stim)}, {lit(stem)}, {lit(b['correctAnswer'])}, true, {lit(NOTE)} FROM lr_sections s WHERE s.preptest={pt} AND s.section={sec} AND NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM lr_questions q WHERE q.qid={lit(slot)}) ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING;")
            for L in "ABCDE":
                tag = d["notes"][L][0]
                sql.append(f"INSERT INTO lr_answer_choices (question_id, letter, choice_text, tag, is_correct) SELECT q.id, {lit(L)}, {lit(norm_text(b['answerChoices'][L]))}, {lit(tag)}, {'true' if L == b['correctAnswer'] else 'false'} FROM lr_questions q WHERE q.qid={lit(slot)} AND NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM lr_answer_choices c WHERE c.question_id=q.id AND c.letter={lit(L)}) ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING;")
            target = "e.style='single'"
            new_expl = True
        else:
            exps = exq[q["id"]]
            sql.append(f"DO $guard$ BEGIN IF NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM lr_questions q WHERE q.qid={lit(slot)} AND q.stimulus={lit(q['stimulus'])} AND q.prompt={lit(q['prompt'])} AND q.correct_choice={lit(q['correct_choice'])}) THEN RAISE EXCEPTION 'Question changed: {slot}'; END IF; END $guard$;")
            if q["correct_choice"] != d["key"]:
                errors.append(f"{slot}: DB key differs")
            if not exps:
                state, new_expl, target = "unexplained", True, "e.style='single'"
                sql.append(f"DO $guard$ BEGIN IF (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM lr_explanations e JOIN lr_questions q ON q.id=e.question_id WHERE q.qid={lit(slot)} AND e.style IN ('single','standard')) <> 0 THEN RAISE EXCEPTION 'Explanation row changed: {slot}'; END IF; END $guard$;")
            else:
                if len(exps) != 1:
                    errors.append(f"{slot}: {len(exps)} explanation rows")
                    continue
                e = exps[0]
                have = sorted(nt[e["id"]])
                new_expl, target = False, f"e.id={lit(e['id'])}"
                missing = [L for L in "ABCDE" if L not in have]
                if sorted(d["notes"]) != missing:
                    errors.append(f"{slot}: draft letters {sorted(d['notes'])} vs missing {missing}")
                if st[e["id"]]:
                    state = "partial" if have else "notes-only"
                    if d["steps"]:
                        errors.append(f"{slot}: steps already exist")
                else:
                    state = "steps-only" if not missing else "steps-and-notes"
                    if len(d["steps"]) < 3:
                        errors.append(f"{slot}: needs steps")
                    vals = ", ".join(f"({i}, {lit(l)}, {lit(bd)})" for i, (l, bd) in enumerate(d["steps"]))
                    stepsql = f"INSERT INTO lr_explanation_steps (explanation_id, position, label, body) SELECT e.id, v.position, v.label, v.body FROM lr_explanations e CROSS JOIN (VALUES {vals}) v(position,label,body) WHERE {target} AND NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM lr_explanation_steps s WHERE s.explanation_id=e.id) ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING;"
                sql.append(f"DO $guard$ BEGIN IF (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM lr_explanation_steps s WHERE s.explanation_id={lit(e['id'])}) <> {len(st[e['id']])} OR (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM lr_explanation_choice_notes n WHERE n.explanation_id={lit(e['id'])}) <> {len(have)} THEN RAISE EXCEPTION 'Explanation changed: {slot}'; END IF; END $guard$;")
        if new_expl:
            sql.append(f"INSERT INTO lr_explanations (question_id, style) SELECT q.id, 'single' FROM lr_questions q WHERE q.qid={lit(slot)} AND NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM lr_explanations e WHERE e.question_id=q.id AND e.style IN ('single','standard')) ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING;")
            vals = ", ".join(f"({i}, {lit(l)}, {lit(bd)})" for i, (l, bd) in enumerate(d["steps"]))
            sql.append(f"INSERT INTO lr_explanation_steps (explanation_id, position, label, body) SELECT e.id, v.position, v.label, v.body FROM lr_explanations e JOIN lr_questions q ON q.id=e.question_id CROSS JOIN (VALUES {vals}) v(position,label,body) WHERE q.qid={lit(slot)} AND {target} AND NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM lr_explanation_steps s WHERE s.explanation_id=e.id) ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING;")
        if not new_expl and state in ("steps-only", "steps-and-notes"):
            sql.append(stepsql)
        nvals = ", ".join(f"({lit(L)}, {lit(d['notes'][L][1])})" for L in sorted(d["notes"]))
        if d["notes"]:
            sql.append(f"INSERT INTO lr_explanation_choice_notes (explanation_id, letter, body) SELECT e.id, v.letter, v.body FROM lr_explanations e JOIN lr_questions q ON q.id=e.question_id CROSS JOIN (VALUES {nvals}) v(letter,body) WHERE q.qid={lit(slot)} AND {target} AND NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM lr_explanation_choice_notes n WHERE n.explanation_id=e.id AND n.letter=v.letter) ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING;")
        sql.append("COMMIT;")
        fname = f"batch{BATCH}-{slot.lower()}.sql"
        (OUT / fname).write_text("\n".join(sql) + "\n", encoding="utf-8")
        manifest.append(dict(slot=slot, file=fname, state=state, steps=len(d["steps"]), notes=len(d["notes"])))
    (OUT / f"batch{BATCH}-sql-manifest.json").write_text(json.dumps(manifest, indent=2), encoding="utf-8")
    if errors:
        print("ERRORS:\n" + "\n".join(errors))
        sys.exit(1)
    from collections import Counter
    print(f"{len(manifest)} slots", Counter(m["state"] for m in manifest), "steps", sum(m["steps"] for m in manifest), "notes", sum(m["notes"] for m in manifest))


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Situation / Evaluate / Goal when the answers generalize a situation.\n\n## PT102-S4-Q5 | Principle-Strengthen | A\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Conclusion:** Zimbabwe objects to a total ban on ivory trading.\n\n**Evidence:** Zimbabwe has virtually eliminated poaching inside its borders and earns income by culling herds that grow too big. Zimbabwe says the problem is not the ivory trade but other countries' conservation policies.\n\n**Evaluate:** Zimbabwe's case is that it isn't the cause of the problem, yet a worldwide ban would cost it the culling income. So the principle has to say that a fix aimed at a problem shouldn't hurt the countries that didn't cause it.\n\n**Goal:** If a country is not responsible for a problem, international measures to correct that problem should not harm it.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) Correct.** Zimbabwe isn't responsible for the poaching, and a ban would take away its culling income. This principle says a measure like that shouldn't adversely affect a country in Zimbabwe's position, which is exactly the basis for its objection.\n\n**(B) Out of Scope.** Where the right to trade comes from never comes up. If anything, saying trade rests on agreements among nations makes a ban agreed by nations easier to defend.\n\n**(C) Not Described.** Zimbabwe never argues from sovereignty. Its point is that it isn't the source of the problem, and it doesn't say saving the elephant matters less than anything.\n\n**(D) Out of Scope.** Who should enforce a ban is beside the point. Zimbabwe objects to having the ban at all.\n\n**(E) Doesn't Support.** This is the most-picked wrong answer because it sounds like Zimbabwe's record. But it says only that eliminating poaching is necessary for conservation. It gives no reason why a country that already did so should escape a ban, so it doesn't support the objection.\n\n## PT105-S1-Q24 | Principle-Strengthen | D\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Conclusion:** The government should pay for candidates' campaigns.\n\n**Evidence:** Society has a vested interest in a system where candidates are free to keep their principles. Campaigns are costly, so most candidates seek private money, and that almost always forces them to compromise.\n\n**Evaluate:** Government funding would keep candidates free of private money, which serves society's vested interest. The principle needs to link \"serves a vested interest of society\" to \"government should pay for it.\"\n\n**Goal:** If government financing of an activity furthers a vested interest of society, the government should finance it.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) Wrong Conclusion.** This tells candidates not to run. The argument concludes that the government should pay, and this principle says nothing about who pays.\n\n**(B) Wrong Conclusion.** This limits fundraising by wealthy candidates. The conclusion is about government paying for campaigns, and wealthy candidates are not the problem the editorial describes.\n\n**(C) Wrong Conclusion.** This is advice to voters. It does nothing to show that government should cover the costs.\n\n**(D) Correct.** Paying for campaigns frees candidates from private funders, which protects the system society has a vested interest in. This principle turns that fact into the conclusion that the government should pay.\n\n**(E) Wrong Conclusion.** This sets a condition for encouraging private funding. The argument wants to replace private funding with public money, and this principle never mentions the government.\n\n## PT105-S4-Q7 | Principle-Strengthen | E\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Conclusion:** Lopez and Simmons are foolish to reveal their marriage to Evritech.\n\n**Evidence:** Revealing it means one of them loses the job offer. They could keep the marriage secret and later say they married after being hired, and Evritech has no policy of firing one of two employees who marry.\n\n**Evaluate:** Willett's advice only works if the couple is allowed to stay quiet about their marriage. If they had a duty to tell Evritech, keeping it secret wouldn't be a sensible option. The principle has to remove that duty.\n\n**Goal:** Job candidates have no obligation to tell an employer about their marriage.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) Wrong Conclusion.** This is about what Evritech's policy should be. Willett is advising the couple about what to do under the policy that exists.\n\n**(B) Wrong Conclusion.** This says when a company should adopt the one-family rule. It doesn't help show that the couple is foolish to disclose.\n\n**(C) Opposite.** If withholding requested information is as bad as lying, the couple shouldn't keep quiet. That undercuts Willett's advice.\n\n**(D) Wrong Conclusion.** This says candidates who can't follow a company's policies should turn down the job. It points toward the couple declining, not toward keeping the marriage secret.\n\n**(E) Correct.** If candidates don't have to reveal marital status whatever the employer's policies, then the couple can keep quiet without doing anything wrong. That makes revealing the marriage, and losing an offer, the foolish choice.\n\n## PT110-S2-Q20 | Principle-Strengthen | D\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Conclusion:** The literature department should include the study of advertisements in its undergraduate courses.\n\n**Evidence:** Advertisements have a harmful effect on society, largely because people can't discern their real messages. The reasoning is about harm and skills: ads do damage that the department's skills could counter. The principle should link \"a text that can harm society\" to \"the department's courses should equip students to analyze it.\"\n\n**Goal:** If a text could harm society, the literature department's courses should help students analyze and understand it.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) Wrong Conclusion.** This tells advertisers how to write ads. Wigmore's conclusion is about what the literature department should teach.\n\n**(B) Wrong Approach.** Wigmore explicitly sets aside whether ads are literature (\"might or might not be\"). It doesn't say that course has to cover advertisements, or that the literature department should teach it.\n\n**(D) Correct.** Ads are texts that harm society, and this principle says the department's courses ought to equip students to analyze any such text. That cuts against Wigmore.\n\n## PT111-S1-Q2 | Principle-Strengthen | D\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Conclusion:** The generalization is correctly regarded as a scientific law.\n\n**Evidence:** No test available with current knowledge and technology has falsified it. The principle must say that this much is enough to count as a scientific law.\n\n**Goal:** If a generalization is confirmed to the extent current science allows, it is a scientific law.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) Reversed.** This says scientific laws haven't been falsified. The generalization has been tested under far more than a few circumstances, so the principle doesn't apply, and it can't establish that something is a law anyway.\n\n**(C) Wrong Conclusion.** This is about what will eventually be confirmed. It doesn't say anything about what counts as a scientific law.\n\n**(D) Correct.** The generalization is confirmed as far as current science allows. Plug that into this principle and you get that it's considered a scientific law, which is the lecturer's classification.\n\n**(E) Wrong Direction.** This starts from \"regarded as a law\" and predicts future verification. The comparison is cheap tool against expensive tool.\n\n**(C) Irrelevant Comparison.** Whether spending is as justified for civilian as for military training doesn't matter. The argument says spending is often not justified in either.\n\n**(D) Correct.** In both studies a cheaper tool was at least as effective. This principle says not to buy the expensive aid in that situation, so you shouldn't always buy high-tech tools.\n\n**(E) Out of Scope.** Learning styles and variety never come up. The studies compare cost and results, nothing else.\n\n## PT112-S4-Q23 | Principle-Strengthen | C\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Conclusion:** Journalists are as much to blame as the director for inflating the charity's reputation.\n\n**Evidence:** The journalists naively accepted the director's numbers and reported them as fact.\n\n**Evaluate:** The journalists didn't lie. They passed along false numbers without checking. The principle has to make reporting an unverified story as fact just as blameworthy as the original deception.\n\n**Goal:** Anyone who reports an unverified story as fact is as responsible for its effects as anyone else.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) Doesn't Apply.** This covers people who work for a charity. The journalists don't, so it can't spread the blame to them.\n\n**(B) Doesn't Apply.** This covers people who knowingly help a liar hide the truth. The journalists were naive, not knowing accomplices.\n\n**(C) Correct.** The journalists reported the numbers as fact without trying to verify them, and the story turned out to be false. This principle says they are no less responsible than anyone else, including the director. That is the investigators' claim.\n\n**(D) Out of Scope.** This ranks liars by motive. The journalists didn't lie, and the claim is about how blame is shared, not about motives.\n\n**(E) Wrong Conclusion.** This would make the director less blameworthy for accepting responsibility. It says nothing about the journalists being equally to blame.\n\n## PT114-S1-Q3 | Principle-Strengthen | E\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Conclusion:** The chemist charges the anthropologist with fraud.\n\n**Evidence:** The anthropologist's hypothesis was that the powder contained toxin T. All we have is a scientist who failed to report a result that went against the hypothesis. The principle has to make that failure fraud.\n\n**Goal:** Scientists who fail to report a disconfirming result commit fraud.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) Doesn't Apply.** Both cases here involve reporting something. The anthropologist reported nothing, so neither kind of fraud fits.\n\n**(B) Doesn't Support.** This says fraud is possible even when some disconfirmations are reported. It doesn't say that failing to report one is fraud.\n\n**(C) Opposite.** This says a scientist can leave out disconfirmations and still be innocent. That helps the anthropologist, not the chemist.\n\n**(D) Doesn't Apply.** This is about reporting a result one knows is invalid. The anthropologist reported nothing.\n\n**(E) Correct.** The anthropologist left out a test that could be read as disconfirming the hypothesis. This principle says that is fraud, so it supports the charge.\n\n## PT114-S2-Q4 | Principle-Strengthen | E\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Conclusion:** The biographer's ignorance can't be used to escape blame for misleading readers.\n\n**Evidence:** The omitted facts were available to anyone when the book was written. The principle should blame a biographer for misleading omissions of facts that were widely available.\n\n**Goal:** A biographer should be blamed for misperceptions caused by omitting facts that were widely available.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) Out of Scope.** Whether readers see the book as favorable isn't the issue. The issue is blame for misleading readers through omission.\n\n**(B) Opposite.** This blames biographers only when omissions are deliberate. The author says the omission was not deliberate, so this lets the author off.\n\n**(C) Doesn't Apply.** These facts were incriminating, so they wouldn't have supported a favorable view. And this principle excuses authors rather than blaming them.\n\n**(D) Opposite.** This blames authors only for facts no one else had. The facts here were available to anyone, so the principle clears the author.\n\n**(E) Correct.** The facts were widely available, and leaving them out misled readers. This principle says the biographer should be blamed for that, whatever the author knew.\n\n## PT118-S4-Q3 | Principle-Strengthen | E\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Conclusion:** It's a mistake to think nylon is not natural.\n\n**Evidence:** Nylon's main components come from petroleum and from nitrogen in the atmosphere. The principle has to say that natural origins for the main components make a substance natural.\n\n**Goal:** If a substance's main components have natural origins, the substance is natural.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) Out of Scope.** The spokesperson never discusses what nylon is used for. The argument is about where its components come from.\n\n**(B) Out of Scope.** This is about production processes. The spokesperson talks only about the sources of the components.\n\n**(C) Doesn't Support.** This caps a substance's naturalness at its least natural component. It could only show that nylon is no more natural than petroleum. It never says when something counts as natural.\n\n**(D) Wrong Conclusion.** This is about one substance being more natural than another. The spokesperson isn't saying nylon is more natural than cotton, only that it is natural.\n\n**(E) Correct.** Nylon's main components come from the atmosphere and from petroleum, and both have natural origins. This principle says that makes nylon natural.\n\n## PT119-S2-Q5 | Principle-Strengthen | A\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Conclusion:** Art in the next century will rarely express views seen as subversive of the social order.\n\n**Evidence:** Artists will be supported mostly by private patrons, and these patrons will almost always support the social order.\n\n**Evaluate:** The futurist jumps from what patrons believe to what art will say. The gap is that patrons won't fund art that opposes their views. The principle needs to supply that.\n\n**Goal:** Patrons tend not to support artists whose work opposes the patrons' views.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) Correct.** Patrons will support the social order, and this principle says they won't back art that opposes their views. So subversive art won't get funding, and it will rarely be made.\n\n**(B) Doesn't Support.** If patrons don't care about social issues, their politics wouldn't shape what they fund. That weakens the futurist's link.\n\n**(C) Doesn't Support.** If artists attack and defend the order equally, we'd expect plenty of subversive art unless something stops it. This gives no reason patrons would stop it.\n\n**(D) Wrong Situation.** This is about artists freed from patrons. In the futurist's next century, artists depend on patrons.\n\n**(E) Opposite.** If patrons don't oppose change started by artists, they might fund subversive art. It also only limits who else should give aid.\n\n**(B) Correct.** Germany alone has the capacity to revive its neighbors' economies. This principle says a nation in that position should use it, so Germany should begin aiding them.\n\n**(C) Doesn't Apply.** Nothing says Germany can afford to help only a few nations. This principle doesn't match the facts.\n\n**(D) Reversed.** \"Only nations that alone have the capacity should exercise it\" makes having that capacity necessary for acting, not sufficient. It tells us who may act, not that Germany must. Picked by 9 percent, so watch for \"only\" turning a trigger into a requirement.\n\n**(E) Reversed.** Like D, \"only\" makes the condition necessary. It also depends on Germany being able to aid just a few nations, which we aren't told.\n\n## PT120-S1-Q3 | Principle-Strengthen | C\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Conclusion:** The chair refuses to sign the letter protesting the lawmaker's interference.\n\n**Evidence:** The chair had earlier written a letter praising the same lawmaker for publicizing a senseless military expenditure.\n\n**Evaluate:** The chair's ground is consistency: having praised the lawmaker for acting as a watchdog in one case, the chair can't protest the same watchdog role now. The principle has to say that, even when the new case touches the chair's own field.\n\n**Goal:** Someone who praised a lawmaker for being a watchdog in one case shouldn't criticize the lawmaker for doing the same in a case involving that person's own professional interests.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) Wrong Conclusion.** This asks for careful thought before criticizing. It doesn't tell the chair not to criticize, and the chair's reason is the earlier praise, not a lack of care.\n\n**(B) Out of Scope.** The chair's duty over department funds isn't the reason given. The chair privately backs the project anyway.\n\n**(C) Correct.** The chair praised the lawmaker's watchdog role over the military spending. Now the lawmaker plays the same role in the chair's own department. This principle says the chair shouldn't criticize the lawmaker in that situation.\n\n**(D) Wrong Reason.** This bars academics from judging government officials because of taxes and funding. The chair's stated ground is the earlier letter, not tax status.\n\n**(E) Out of Scope.** This compares universities and the military on spending wisely. It doesn't explain why the chair, having praised the lawmaker, shouldn't sign.\n\n## PT121-S1-Q18 | Principle-Strengthen | D\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Conclusion:** If owning gasoline-powered cars is wrong, you shouldn't own your electric car either.\n\n**Evidence:** The company that made your electric car also makes millions of gasoline cars, so buying from it supports a maker of products you object to.\n\n**Evaluate:** The advocate's point is about support. Buying the car supports a company that does what you think is wrong. The principle has to say that you shouldn't support an organization that does something you believe is wrong.\n\n**Goal:** One should not support an organization that does anything one believes is wrong.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) Doesn't Support.** This says a less harmful action can still be wrong. The advocate's reason isn't that the electric car is harmful. It's that buying it supports the gasoline-car maker.\n\n**(B) Doesn't Apply.** The electric car pollutes far less, so this principle would, if anything, approve of buying it.\n\n**(C) Out of Scope.** This says when one should buy something. The advocate is arguing about when one should not.\n\n**(D) Correct.** If you think gasoline cars are wrong, the company does something you believe is wrong. Owning its electric car supports it, so by this principle you shouldn't own it.\n\n**(E) Doesn't Apply.** This targets companies with no sound products. This company makes the electric car, which pollutes far less, so the principle doesn't reach it.\n\n## PT122-S1-Q17 | Principle-Strengthen | C\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Conclusion:** Sharon did nothing wrong in buying the vase.\n\n**Evidence:** The storekeeper priced the vase at $10 and Sharon paid $10. Sharon knew it was worth at least $1,000 and said nothing.\n\n**Evaluate:** The only thing Sharon did for the storekeeper was pay the asking price. To call that blameless, the principle has to say the buyer's only duty is to pay what the seller asks. Then saying nothing about the value breaks no duty.\n\n**Goal:** A buyer's only obligation is to pay the price the seller asks.\n\n## PT122-S2-Q15 | Principle-Strengthen | C\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Conclusion:** Careful safety testing is even more important for household products than for prescription medicines.\n\n**Evidence:** The more people regularly use a product, the more people's health it may put at risk. More people regularly use household maintenance products than prescription medicines.\n\n**Evaluate:** The evidence ends with \"more people at risk from household products.\" The conclusion is \"more important to test household products.\" The principle has to link the two: more people at risk means testing matters more.\n\n**Goal:** The more people whose health a product might put at risk, the more important it is to test that product.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) Too Weak.** \"Depends mainly on\" says the number of users is the key factor, but not which way it cuts. It doesn't say that more users make testing more important.\n\n**(B) Not Comparative.** This says testing is very important for widely used products. The conclusion compares household products with medicines, and this principle could make both equally important.\n\n**(C) Correct.** Household products put more people at risk than prescription medicines. This principle says that makes testing them more important, which is the conclusion.\n\n**(D) Out of Scope.** This compares two medicines by dosing frequency. The argument compares household products with medicines by number of users.\n\n**(E) Roundabout.** This sets medicines as generally more important unless a nonmedical product puts more people at risk. The exception gets you to the conclusion, but the principle adds a general rule that points the other way. C states the needed link directly.\n\n## PT137-S4-Q7 | Principle-Strengthen | E\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Conclusion:** MacArthur should not have published the diet book.\n\n**Evidence:** The book recommended so few fruits and vegetables that it damaged many followers' health. As a physician, MacArthur knew or should have known that such diets are unhealthful.\n\n**Evaluate:** The evidence gives two facts: publishing recommended an unhealthful course, and MacArthur knew or should have known it. The principle must turn those two facts into \"should not have published.\"\n\n**Goal:** One should not publish a book recommending a course of action that one knows, or should know, is unhealthful.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) Too Narrow.** This needs MacArthur to know the book would seriously damage health. The argument says only that MacArthur knew or should have known. If MacArthur merely should have known, the principle doesn't apply.\n\n**(B) Wrong Target.** This tells people not to follow a harmful method. The conclusion is about whether MacArthur should have published, not what readers should do.\n\n**(C) Opposite Direction.** This says when one should publish. The argument concludes that MacArthur shouldn't have, and this principle can't get there.\n\n**(D) Doesn't Apply.** This is about knowing the means will reach the goal. The book did help people lose weight, so the problem isn't that it failed. The problem is the harm to health.\n\n**(E) Correct.** MacArthur published a book recommending a diet that was unhealthful, and either knew or ought to have known it. This principle says not to publish in that situation, so MacArthur shouldn't have.\n\n## PT141-S2-Q4 | Principle-Conform | D\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Rule:** If an executive's compensation is set by the advice of an outside consultant, and that consultant also has business interests with the company, the executive is likely overcompensated.\n\n**Evaluate:** The rule has two triggers that must both be present: the pay was set on an outside consultant's advice, and that consultant has other business with the company. The outcome is \"likely\" overcompensated, not \"definitely.\" The rule says nothing about cases where a trigger is missing.\n\n**Goal:** A judgment that an executive is probably overpaid because the pay was set by an outside consultant who has other business with the company.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) No Trigger.** There's no consultant here, just a comparison with average pay. And \"definitely\" overstates the rule's \"likely.\"\n\n**(B) No Trigger.** Pay set during a profitable year has nothing to do with an outside consultant with ties to the company.\n\n**(C) Illegal Negation.** No outside consultant means the rule's trigger is missing. The rule then tells us nothing, so it can't support \"probably not overpaid.\"\n\n**(D) Correct.** The board followed an outside consultant's advice, and that consultant has many other contracts with Troskco. Both triggers are present, and \"probably overpaid\" matches the rule's \"likely.\"\n\n**(E) Illegal Negation.** The consultant has no other contracts, so one trigger fails and the rule says nothing. \"Definitely not overpaid\" also goes far beyond anything the rule could support.\n\n## PT141-S4-Q4 | Method | C\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Conclusion:** The argument that Freudian therapy is most effective because it is difficult and time consuming doesn't follow.\n\n**Evidence:** The same reasoning would conclude that a car-repair chain is the most effective because the cars it services get so much work and spend so much time in the shop, and no one would accept that.\n\n**Evaluate:** The psychologist doesn't attack the premises. The psychologist builds an argument with the same structure, effort and time so effectiveness, whose conclusion is plainly unacceptable. If the copy fails, so does the original.\n\n**Goal:** The psychologist presents a parallel argument with an obviously false conclusion.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) Not Described.** The psychologist never offers a competing principle. The attack is through a parallel example.\n\n**(B) Not Described.** The psychologist doesn't question that Freudian therapy is difficult and time consuming. The attack is on the inference.\n\n**(C) Correct.** The car-repair argument has the same structure as the Freudian argument, and its conclusion is one \"that would never be accepted.\" Undermining an argument with a parallel that leads to an obviously false conclusion is exactly this.\n\n**(D) Not Described.** The original argument doesn't use an analogy. The psychologist is the one who introduces the comparison.\n\n**(E) Not Described.** The psychologist never says the difficulty is caused by effectiveness. There's no reversal of cause and effect.\n\n## PT146-S3-Q6 | Principle-Strengthen | B\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Conclusion:** One should not regret the missed opportunities of youth.\n\n**Evidence:** Had one seized those opportunities, one wouldn't have some of one's current close relationships. Everyone deeply cherishes their close relationships.\n\n**Evaluate:** Passing up those opportunities helped bring about relationships one cherishes. The principle has to say that a decision that helped bring about something one cherishes is not one to regret.\n\n**Goal:** One should not regret a decision that helped bring about something one cherishes.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) Doesn't Apply.** This condition turns on whether a different decision would have produced more close relationships. The argument says a different decision would have meant losing some current ones, not that it would have produced more or fewer overall.\n\n**(B) Correct.** Not seizing the opportunities helped bring about relationships one cherishes. This principle says not to regret a decision like that, which gives the conclusion.\n\n**(C) Opposite.** The argument opens by saying decisions can profoundly affect one's life. Missing those opportunities had a big effect, so this principle doesn't apply.\n\n**(D) Wrong Conclusion.** This tells people who have regrets to cherish their relationships more. The conclusion is that one shouldn't have the regrets in the first place.\n\n**(E) Out of Scope.** People with few relationships aren't the subject. The argument is about regret.\n\n## PT147-S4-Q13 | Principle-Conform | C\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Situation:** Emma and John were both careless while playing ball. Only John's throw broke the window, so only John should have to make it up to the neighbor.\n\n**Evaluate:** Both were equally careless, so carelessness alone doesn't decide who pays. What decides it is who actually caused the damage. The principle: when two people share the carelessness, the one whose action caused the damage should compensate, and the other shouldn't.\n\n**Goal:** Two people are both careless, only one causes the damage, and only that one has to pay.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) No Damage.** Only one person is involved, and nothing bad happened. The principle is about assigning the cost of actual damage between two careless people.\n\n**(B) No Fault.** Linda and Seung were careful, and nothing was damaged. This is about paying for work, not compensating for harm.\n\n**(C) Correct.** Terry and Chris were both racing on a public street, so both were careless. Chris alone hit the parked car, and Chris alone pays. That matches John and Emma exactly.\n\n**(D) Wrong Party.** Alexis and Juan didn't cause the problem, and the one who did isn't the one paying. The owner refunds them for damage done by someone else.\n\n**(E) Different Question.** This is about whether the careless person can make the owner pay. In the stimulus, one of the careless people pays the neighbor.\n\n## PT154-S2-Q4 | Principle-Strengthen | D\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Conclusion:** Programs that pay children to read should be viewed with suspicion.\n\n**Evidence:** Paying children to read might get them to read more, but it might teach them to see reading as a chore rather than a source of satisfaction.\n\n**Evaluate:** The editorial notes a possible effect but never says why it's bad. To get to \"suspicion,\" the principle has to make instilling enjoyment of reading the program's goal. Then a program that might teach children to see reading as a chore works against that goal.\n\n**Goal:** The goal of early reading programs should be to instill a love of reading.\n"}
