{"file_path": "C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/corpus/lr-explanations/drafts/batch-10-mc-part-b.md", "content": "---\ntype: draft\nsubject: LR batch 10, part B, 20 slots (Main Conclusion, one Role)\nstatus: draft\ncreated: 2026-10-02\n---\n\n# Batch 10, part B: Main Conclusion\n\nEvery gap with a Main Conclusion label. Nine are in the database with no explanation. Eleven are not in the database, so loading them inserts the question and choices from the bank.\n\nTwo unloaded slots carry a wrong bank label: PT147-S4-Q17 (\"Identify The Conclusion\") loads as Main Conclusion, and PT156-S4-Q6 loads as Role, because its stem asks for the role of a statement.\n\nModelled on `models/MC-Role-Agree.md`: find the claim everything else supports, swap a referential phrase for what it refers to, and name the role of each wrong answer (premise, intermediate conclusion, opposing view).\n\nLabels: Conclusion / Evidence / Evaluate / Goal, with Opposing Point where the argument answers someone.\n\n## PT107-S4-Q6 | Main Conclusion | E\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Opposing Point:** Some judges complain that mandatory minimum sentences are too mechanical and stop judges from deciding who can be rehabilitated.\n\n**Conclusion:** That is exactly why mandatory minimum sentences are necessary.\n\n**Evidence:** People free to use their own judgment think they act wisely but are often arbitrary and irrational, and judges are no exception.\n\n**Evaluate:** \"But that is precisely why\" marks the pivot. Swap \"that\" for what it refers to: minimums are necessary because they limit judges' discretion. The last two sentences explain why limiting discretion is good: judges' judgment is often arbitrary.\n\n**Goal:** Mandatory minimum sentences are needed because they keep judges from acting arbitrarily.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) Too Strong Premise.** This is a distorted version of the evidence. The passage says people are often arbitrary, not that they never have good judgment.\n\n**(B) Opposing Point.** This is the judges' complaint, which the author is answering.\n\n**(C) Opposite.** The author argues that judges' discretion should be limited.\n\n**(D) Premise.** This is the support for the conclusion. Judges being often arbitrary is why minimums are needed.\n\n**(E) Correct.** Minimums are necessary, and the reason is to limit arbitrary judicial discretion. This combines the pivot sentence with what \"that\" refers to.\n\n## PT110-S2-Q18 | Main Conclusion | B\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Opposing Point:** Radical environmentalists' positions often contain false hypotheses and economically infeasible proposals.\n\n**Conclusion:** Even these extremists play a positive role.\n\n**Evidence:** Inertia on environmental issues is so stubborn that progress happens only if people fear environmental disaster, however ill-founded the fear.\n\n**Evaluate:** \"But there is a positive role\" is the pivot, and \"for\" introduces the support. What is the positive role? Radicals create the fear that makes progress possible. So the conclusion is that radicals, by pushing their views, help bring about progress.\n\n**Goal:** Radical environmentalists play a positive role by stimulating progress.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) Too Strong.** The editorialist doesn't say how much progress has been made, or that it is mainly due to radicals. The claim is only that radicals play a positive role.\n\n**(B) Correct.** This states the positive role: by promoting their views, radicals help produce progress. That is the claim the \"for\" clause supports.\n\n**(C) Premise, Distorted.** This restates the evidence about fear and inertia, and adds \"most effectively,\" which the passage doesn't say.\n\n**(D) Wrong Motive.** This is the most-picked trap. It says radicals put forth untenable positions in order to produce fear. The editorialist never says the radicals intend this. They have a positive effect, whatever their motives.\n\n**(E) Opposing Point, Exaggerated.** This overstates the concession at the start. It's the part the author grants before the \"but.\"\n\n## PT114-S4-Q3 | Main Conclusion | E\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Opposing Point:** Obviously no animals would survive without plants.\n\n**Conclusion:** The dependence is mutual.\n\n**Evidence:** Many plant species exist only because animals pollinate, fertilize, and spread their seeds. Without animals, plants' photosynthesis would deplete the atmosphere's carbon dioxide.\n\n**Evaluate:** \"But the dependence is mutual\" is the pivot. Spell out \"mutual\": animals depend on plants, and plants also depend on animals. The two \"Also\" points are evidence for the plant-on-animal side.\n\n**Goal:** Animals and plants depend on each other.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) Too Narrow.** This makes photosynthesis the whole story. The journalist's point covers pollination and seeds too, and this choice also says animals are needed for photosynthesis to occur, which the passage doesn't say.\n\n**(B) Premise.** This is part of the opening concession, not the conclusion.\n\n**(C) Too Narrow.** This picks up only the carbon dioxide point and turns it into a claim about Earth's chemistry. It leaves out the mutual dependence.\n\n**(D) Wrong Detail.** The passage says plants would deplete carbon dioxide, not oxygen. It also isn't the conclusion.\n\n**(E) Correct.** Animals depend on plants, and plants depend on animals. That is what \"the dependence is mutual\" means.\n\n## PT116-S3-Q14 | Main Conclusion | C\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Opposing Point:** Recent studies say the operas of Bizet and Verdi reinforce stereotypes about women in our society.\n\n**Evidence:** Only a small minority of North Americans, opera lovers, have had any significant exposure to these operas.\n\n**Conclusion:** (Unstated) These operas have not significantly contributed to stereotypes about women in contemporary North America.\n\n**Evaluate:** The stem asks for the conclusion the argument is \"structured to establish,\" which hints that it isn't stated outright. The critic answers the studies with \"But only a small minority\" have seen these works. The point being made is that the operas couldn't be shaping our stereotypes much.\n\n**Goal:** The operas have not significantly shaped stereotypes of women in North America today.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) Opposite.** This is roughly what the studies claim. The critic is arguing against it.\n\n**(B) Out of Scope.** The critic never compares nineteenth-century images of women with current ones.\n\n**(C) Correct.** The critic's evidence, that few North Americans have seen the operas, is aimed at showing the operas haven't significantly reinforced stereotypes here. That's the unstated conclusion.\n\n**(D) Too Broad.** The critic talks only about these operas, not opera as a whole as a force on social attitudes.\n\n**(E) Too Strong.** The critic needs only that limited exposure means limited influence. \"Cannot be influenced\" by anything not directly seen goes much further. It's closer to an assumption than to the conclusion.\n\n## PT118-S4-Q1 | Main Conclusion | D\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Opposing Point:** McKinney's policies have often been criticized as benefiting only wealthy residents.\n\n**Conclusion:** That criticism is not a fair evaluation.\n\n**Evidence:** Some of McKinney's policies clearly benefited less affluent residents. McKinney supported lowering property taxes, which spurred development, easing the housing shortage and stabilizing rents.\n\n**Evaluate:** \"But that is not a fair evaluation\" is the conclusion in the first sentence. Everything after it is support, with the tax example backing the claim that some policies helped poorer residents.\n\n**Goal:** The criticism that McKinney's policies benefit only the wealthy is unfair.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) Not Described.** The argument does reach a judgment. It says the criticism is unfair.\n\n**(B) Opposing Point.** This is the criticism the argument rejects.\n\n**(C) Premise.** This is part of the tax example used as support.\n\n**(D) Correct.** The first sentence says the criticism is not fair. \"Unjustified\" means the same.\n\n**(E) Premise.** This is the last link in the evidence chain. It supports the claim that poorer residents benefited.\n\n## PT119-S4-Q9 | Main Conclusion | C\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Evidence:** A free marketplace of ideas gives all ideas a fair hearing, and even prejudiced ideas can do good. Governments release over half the information the public gets through the media.\n\n**Conclusion:** Governments' power over information needs to be curtailed.\n\n**Evidence:** Everyone agrees governments shouldn't suppress free expression, yet they keep building near monopolies on information.\n\n**Evaluate:** \"For this reason\" flags the conclusion in the fourth sentence. The sentences before it and the last sentence all support it.\n\n**Goal:** Governments' control over information should be reduced.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) Not Stated.** The journalist doesn't say the free marketplace is in jeopardy. That's a background worry, not the claim argued for.\n\n**(B) Premise.** The value of a free marketplace is part of the support.\n\n**(C) Correct.** \"Curtailed\" means reduced. This restates the sentence that \"For this reason\" introduces.\n\n**(D) Premise.** This restates the second sentence, which supports the value of a free marketplace.\n\n**(E) Premise.** This is the closing sentence, which adds support for curtailing government power.\n\n## PT120-S3-Q6 | Main Conclusion | A\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Evidence:** Home environment, not breed, mainly determines a dog's disposition. A bad owner can undo generations of breeding.\n\n**Intermediate Conclusion:** Breed-specific legislation wouldn't address how people raise and train dogs.\n\n**Conclusion:** So breed-specific legislation could never effectively protect the public from vicious dogs.\n\n**Evaluate:** \"As a result\" marks the conclusion. The last sentence, that current laws are adequate, is an extra opinion added with \"moreover.\" It doesn't support the claim and isn't supported by it.\n\n**Goal:** Breed-specific legislation can't effectively protect the public from vicious dogs.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) Correct.** This is the sentence \"As a result\" introduces. Nothing else in the argument is supported by it.\n\n**(B) Premise.** This is the citizen's first claim, which supports everything after it. It's also stated a bit too strongly: the citizen calls environment the primary factor, not a \"good\" one specifically.\n\n**(C) Intermediate Conclusion.** This supports the main conclusion. The legislation fails because it can't address home environment.\n\n**(D) Premise.** This restates the second sentence, which supports the claim that environment matters most.\n\n**(E) Wrong Target.** The citizen says breed-specific legislation fails to address human behavior. The current laws are said to be adequate, not to fail at this.\n\n## PT122-S4-Q7 | Main Conclusion | C\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Opposing Point:** The chemicals in treated lumber seem to pose little hazard, because outdoors the fumes can't build up.\n\n**Conclusion:** Steps should be taken right away to determine whether these chemicals are safe.\n\n**Evidence:** Consumers could ingest the chemicals. Children might put their mouths on playground equipment, and chemicals could leach into garden soil.\n\n**Evaluate:** \"However\" turns from the apparent safety to the recommendation. \"Since\" introduces the support, and the last sentence gives the examples of how ingestion might happen.\n\n**Goal:** The safety of the chemicals in treated lumber should be investigated.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) Opposing Point.** This is the view the editorialist turns against with \"However.\"\n\n**(B) Out of Scope.** The editorialist never compares indoor and outdoor use for danger. The point is a different route of exposure.\n\n**(C) Correct.** \"Determine the safety of these chemicals\" means study their effects on people. That's the recommendation after \"However.\"\n\n**(D) Out of Scope.** Children mouthing equipment is an example of how exposure could happen, not advice to parents.\n\n**(E) Too Strong.** The editorialist says only that the safety needs checking. It doesn't claim the lumber is more dangerous than thought, which is why testing is called for.\n\n## PT124-S2-Q18 | Main Conclusion | C\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Opposing Point:** The columnist blames the decline of interest in novels on consumerism, technology, and lazy television viewers.\n\n**Conclusion:** The columnist has overlooked important evidence.\n\n**Evidence:** Contemporary fiction is often poor quality, while many good newspapers, magazines, journals, and other books are available.\n\n**Evaluate:** Tallulah doesn't say the columnist's causes are wrong. Tallulah says the columnist missed something relevant. The last sentence is the evidence that was missed.\n\n**Goal:** The columnist reached that view without considering all the relevant evidence.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) Too Strong.** Tallulah offers the poor quality of fiction as overlooked evidence, not as the proven cause of novels' decline.\n\n**(B) Too Strong.** Tallulah never says the columnist's explanation is false, only that it ignored relevant evidence.\n\n**(C) Correct.** \"Overlooked important evidence\" is the same as forming a view \"without considering all of the pertinent evidence.\"\n\n**(D) Out of Scope.** Tallulah never claims people read as much as before.\n\n**(E) Premise.** This is part of the evidence the columnist overlooked.\n\n## PT127-S1-Q10 | Main Conclusion | D\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Opposing Point:** Some social scientists want predictive power and think they ought to reduce phenomena to math, as the natural sciences have.\n\n**Conclusion:** This would be a mistake.\n\n**Evidence:** Reducing social phenomena to math would neglect data that can't easily be put in mathematical terms, so it would distort the phenomena.\n\n**Evaluate:** \"But this would be a mistake\" is the pivot. Swap \"this\" for what it refers to: social scientists reducing social phenomena to mathematical expressions. The rest is the reason it's a mistake.\n\n**Goal:** Social scientists should not reduce social phenomena to mathematical formulas.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) Not Stated.** The argument doesn't compare how much predictive power each science has.\n\n**(B) Out of Scope.** The argument never ranks math's importance in the two fields.\n\n**(C) Opposing Point.** This is what the social scientists want. The author doesn't argue for it.\n\n**(D) Correct.** With \"this\" filled in, the conclusion says reducing social phenomena to mathematical expressions would be a mistake. That's what D says.\n\n**(E) Too Strong, Background.** The first sentence says prediction is the hallmark of the natural sciences, not that it explains their success. And it's background, not the conclusion.\n\n## PT138-S2-Q19 | Main Conclusion | B\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Conclusion:** Unless the field's focus changes radically, AI research won't produce truly intelligent machines.\n\n**Evidence:** The current focus is on computational ability to the exclusion of everything else. That will produce devices no more truly intelligent than a person with no emotional or other noncognitive responses.\n\n**Evaluate:** The first sentence states the claim, and the second explains why. \"Unless X, not Y\" is the same as \"If not X, then not Y.\"\n\n**Goal:** If AI research's focus is not radically changed, it won't produce truly intelligent machines.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) Premise.** This is the second sentence, the reason the focus must change.\n\n**(B) Correct.** \"Unless the focus is radically changed, research will fail\" translates to \"If the focus is not radically changed, research won't produce true intelligence.\" That's the first sentence.\n\n**(C) Not Stated.** The author says the research will fail, not that it already has.\n\n**(D) Too Strong.** The argument implies noncognitive responses matter for true intelligence. It never says they matter at least as much as computation.\n\n**(E) Assumption, Not Conclusion.** This is closer to something the argument relies on. The conclusion is about what AI research will achieve.\n\n## PT138-S3-Q11 | Main Conclusion | D\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Opposing Point:** Many people assume personal conflicts are inevitable.\n\n**Conclusion:** That assumption is not so: personal conflicts are not inevitable.\n\n**Evidence:** Personal conflicts arise mainly from irrationality. For example, people find it easier to assign someone bad qualities than good ones, even against the evidence.\n\n**Evaluate:** \"But that assumption is just not so\" is the conclusion. Swap \"that assumption\" for its content and you get \"personal conflicts are not inevitable.\" The rest explains why, with an example.\n\n**Goal:** Personal conflicts are not inevitable.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) Opposing Point.** This is the assumption the counselor rejects.\n\n**(B) Premise.** This is the example of irrationality that supports the conclusion.\n\n**(C) Not Stated.** The counselor describes this habit as an example. Calling it irrational in these words isn't the point being argued.\n\n**(D) Correct.** With \"that assumption\" replaced, the counselor's claim is that personal conflicts are not inevitable.\n\n**(E) Premise.** This is the last part of the example, which supports the claim about irrationality.\n\n## PT143-S1-Q8 | Main Conclusion | C\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Opposing Point:** The owners raised rates, saying they needed to hire and train staff for a growing client base.\n\n**Conclusion:** They should reconsider and not raise their rates.\n\n**Evidence:** The company's mission is low-cost gourmet catering, and raising rates would jeopardize it.\n\n**Evaluate:** \"After all\" introduces the support, which points back to the sentence before it as the conclusion.\n\n**Goal:** The catering company should not raise its rates.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) Background.** This is the decision the client is responding to.\n\n**(B) Opposing Point.** This is the owners' reason for the increase, which the client rejects.\n\n**(C) Correct.** This is the client's recommendation, the sentence \"After all\" supports.\n\n**(D) Premise.** This is the first part of the support.\n\n**(E) Premise.** This completes the support. The mission is in danger, so rates shouldn't go up.\n\n## PT147-S4-Q17 | Main Conclusion | A\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Conclusion:** It is pointless to debate the truth of the law of noncontradiction.\n\n**Evidence:** A productive debate requires participants to share some basic principles. In this debate, those shared principles would be less certain than the law itself, so it matters little whether the law can be defended from them.\n\n**Evaluate:** The first sentence makes the claim, and the rest explains why. The \"so\" in the last sentence introduces an intermediate step, which in turn supports the first sentence.\n\n**Goal:** Debating the law of noncontradiction is pointless.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) Correct.** This is the first sentence, which everything else supports.\n\n**(B) Definition.** This is the content of the law, given as background.\n\n**(C) Premise.** This is the first piece of support.\n\n**(D) Not Stated.** The argument never says the law itself must be a shared principle.\n\n**(E) Premise.** This is part of the support for why debating the law is pointless.\n\n## PT148-S3-Q4 | Main Conclusion | A\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Conclusion:** Electric burners would cause fewer fires if their highest temperature were limited to 350°C.\n\n**Evidence:** 350°C is plenty for cooking. Cooking oil and most fibers ignite at 387°C at the lowest, and burners on high go well above 700°C.\n\n**Evaluate:** The first sentence makes the claim. The rest explains why a 350°C limit would prevent fires: it is below the ignition point, yet hot enough to cook.\n\n**Goal:** Limiting burners to 350°C would mean fewer fires.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) Correct.** This is the first sentence, which the other facts support.\n\n**(B) Premise.** This shows the limit wouldn't hurt cooking. It supports the proposal.\n\n**(C) Premise.** This is the ignition threshold the limit stays below.\n\n**(D) Premise.** This shows current burners get hot enough to start fires.\n\n**(E) Not Stated.** The argument doesn't claim to explain why fires start. It says a limit would reduce them.\n\n## PT149-S4-Q3 | Main Conclusion | D\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Conclusion:** The reporter who panned the restaurant is not a true restaurant critic.\n\n**Evidence:** The reporter admits having no special expertise about food and previously covered politics. A newspaper would never call someone without theater training a drama critic.\n\n**Evaluate:** \"He is a good writer, but he is not a true restaurant critic.\" The concession comes first and the conclusion follows \"but.\" The other sentences support it, and the drama critic analogy backs it up.\n\n**Goal:** The reporter is not a true restaurant critic.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) Premise.** This supports the conclusion. No expertise means not a real critic.\n\n**(B) Premise.** This also supports the conclusion.\n\n**(C) Concession.** The owner grants this before the \"but.\"\n\n**(D) Correct.** This is the claim after the \"but\" that the rest supports.\n\n**(E) Premise.** This is an analogy used as support.\n\n## PT156-S2-Q13 | Main Conclusion | E\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Opposing Point:** Santayana recommends studying history to avoid the mistakes of the past.\n\n**Conclusion:** We should not follow that advice.\n\n**Evidence:** History consists of unique, unrepeatable accidents, so none of today's crises are the same as our ancestors' crises. So studying history never enables one to avoid past mistakes.\n\n**Evaluate:** \"But we should not follow his advice\" is the main conclusion. \"For\" introduces the support. The last sentence, beginning \"Thus,\" is an intermediate conclusion that supports the main one. Swap \"his advice\" for its content: we should not study history in order to avoid past mistakes.\n\n**Goal:** One should not study history to try to avoid repeating past mistakes.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) Wrong Reason.** The argument doesn't say studying history leads people to misunderstand the crises they face. It says studying history doesn't help avoid mistakes.\n\n**(B) Premise, Distorted.** This restates the evidence about uniqueness, with periods instead of accidents and crises.\n\n**(C) Premise.** This is the evidence about crises never being the same. \"May appear to be the same\" is also an addition.\n\n**(D) Not Stated.** The author never says studying history is valuable for some other reason.\n\n**(E) Correct.** This is \"we should not follow his advice\" with the advice filled in: don't try to avoid past mistakes by studying history.\n\n## PT156-S4-Q6 | Role | E\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Opposing Point:** Ice-age nomads are commonly portrayed as mainly big-game hunters.\n\n**Conclusion:** Most of their food must have come from other sources, such as small game and plants.\n\n**Evidence:** Communities had only about 15 to 20 members, about the minimum needed to survive. Hunting large animals is dangerous and would risk the lives of several members.\n\n**Evaluate:** The statement in question is introduced as a common portrayal, and \"but\" turns against it. The anthropologist's conclusion is the opposite view. So the statement describes a claim the anthropologist is trying to refute.\n\n**Goal:** The statement describes a view the anthropologist argues against.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) Wrong Role.** The portrayal doesn't support the conclusion. The conclusion contradicts it.\n\n**(B) Wrong Role.** It doesn't clarify a premise. It's the view the premises are used against.\n\n**(C) Wrong Role.** It isn't the anthropologist's objection. It's the theory the anthropologist objects to.\n\n**(D) Wrong Role.** The conclusion is that nomads mostly ate other foods. The portrayal is what that conclusion rejects.\n\n**(E) Correct.** \"Commonly portrayed as\" sets up a popular view, and \"but\" rejects it. The anthropologist is trying to refute it.\n\n## PT159-S3-Q13 | Main Conclusion | A\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Background:** Every human brain contains a mental representation of the external world.\n\n**Conclusion:** This representation necessarily leaves out much detail.\n\n**Evidence:** The external world is much larger and more complex than the brain, and no object can completely represent something larger and more complex than itself.\n\n**Evaluate:** \"Yet\" turns to the claim, and \"After all\" introduces its support. Everything after \"After all\" supports the second sentence.\n\n**Goal:** The brain's representation of the world leaves out much detail.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) Correct.** This is the second sentence, which \"After all\" supports.\n\n**(B) Premise.** This is the first piece of support.\n\n**(C) Premise.** This applies the general rule in the last clause to the brain. It's part of the support.\n\n**(D) Background.** This is the starting fact the conclusion builds on.\n\n**(E) Premise.** This is the general rule in the last clause.\n\n## PT159-S3-Q20 | Main Conclusion | C\n\n{{QUOTE}}\n\n### Argument Breakdown\n\n**Evidence:** Science, like myth, tries to explain our place in the cosmos. The truth of science, like that of myth, is just a question of how well it helps us cope with the human condition.\n\n**Conclusion:** Science falls short of complete truth.\n\n**Evidence:** Science is responsible for the breakdown of society's key institutions, which shows it doesn't help us cope well.\n\n**Evaluate:** \"So\" introduces the conclusion in the last sentence. \"While science undoubtedly can count some successes\" is a concession, and the main claim is that science \"falls short of complete truth.\" The breakdown of institutions is the evidence for that.\n\n**Goal:** Science is only partially true.\n\n### Choice Notes\n\n**(A) Premise.** This is the evidence used to show science falls short.\n\n**(B) Too Strong.** The essayist compares science to myth in one respect, not in how true it is.\n\n**(C) Correct.** Some successes, but short of complete truth, means only partially true.\n\n**(D) Too Strong.** The essayist says science is like myth in one respect. Saying it is merely a myth goes further.\n\n**(E) Premise.** This is the comparison in the first two sentences, which supports the conclusion.\n"}

# Read-only REST pull of the LR question + explanation tables. Run from the vault root.
# Usage: python system/scripts/lsatlab-pull/db_pull.py [out_dir] [table ...]  (default: data/db next to this file; the four LR explanation tables)
import sys
from pathlib import Path
sys.path.insert(0, str(Path(__file__).resolve().parents[1]))
from project_paths import project_path, VAULT_ROOT
import sys
import json, re, urllib.request, os
env={}
for f in [VAULT_ROOT / '.env', project_path('lsatu', '.env')]:
  for l in open(f,encoding='utf-8'):
    m=re.match(r'([A-Z_]+)=(.*)',l.strip())
    if m: env[m[1]]=m[2].strip('"')
U=env['VITE_SUPABASE_URL'];K=env['VITE_SUPABASE_PUBLISHABLE_KEY']
def req(p,body=None,tok=None):
  r=urllib.request.Request(U+p,data=json.dumps(body).encode() if body else None,headers={'apikey':[REDACTED],'Content-Type':'application/json','Authorization':[REDACTED] or K)})
  return json.load(urllib.request.urlopen(r))
tok=req('/auth/v1/token?grant_type=password',{'email':env['LSATPREP_ADMIN_EMAIL'],'password':[REDACTED]})['access_token']
out=sys.argv[1] if len(sys.argv)>1 else os.path.join(os.path.dirname(os.path.abspath(__file__)),'data','db')
os.makedirs(out,exist_ok=True)
for t in (sys.argv[2:] or ['lr_questions','lr_explanations','lr_explanation_steps','lr_explanation_choice_notes']):
  rows=[];o=0
  while True:
    b=req(f'/rest/v1/{t}?select=*&limit=1000&offset={o}',tok=tok);rows+=b;o+=1000
    if len(b)<1000:break
  json.dump(rows,open(os.path.join(out,t+'.json'),'w',encoding='utf-8'),ensure_ascii=False)
  print(t,len(rows), sorted(rows[0].keys()) if rows else '')

INSERT INTO lr_explanation_choice_notes (explanation_id, letter, body) SELECT e.id, v.letter, v.body FROM lr_explanations e JOIN lr_questions q ON q.id=e.question_id CROSS JOIN (VALUES ($batch10$A$batch10$, $batch10$This tells advertisers how to write ads. ## [2026-10-02] draft | Conditional bootcamp opening lessons

## user

Base directory for this skill: C:\Users\Joshu\.claude\skills\session-handoff

# Session Handoff

Produce a repeatable end-of-session summary so Joshua can `/clear` and start a fresh agent without losing continuity. The next agent should pick up by reading this summary alone.

This is a **context-handoff artifact**, not a status report. The audience is a future instance of you, not a stakeholder.

**Handoff vs. wrapup:** run `session-handoff` when the *context window* is the problem — nearing ~250k tokens (the dumb zone), or passing work between Claude and Codex. It is chat-only and writes nothing. Run `wrapup` when the *work day* is done — it strikes tasks, refreshes `wiki/hot.md`, and appends `wiki/log.md`. They can be run back to back (wrapup to persist, then handoff to reset).

## When to invoke

Joshua says: "session handoff", "hand off", "handoff summary", "summarize before I clear", or any near-equivalent. Note "let's wrap up" belongs to `wrapup`, not here — if he says that and the context window is also deep, run `wrapup` first, then offer this. Also invoke proactively if he says he's about to `/clear` without having run it, or if the session is clearly deep into the dumb zone.

## How to produce the summary

1. **Review the full conversation**, not just the last few turns. Handoffs miss things when they only summarize recent context.
2. **Pull state from these sources (in order):**
   - Plan files referenced this session (check `~/.claude/plans/` if a plan was mentioned).
   - TodoWrite state — any in-progress or pending tasks.
   - Background processes started with `run_in_background` — shell IDs are load-bearing for the next agent.
   - Files created or modified this session — you know what you touched; don't grep to re-discover.
   - Memory files written or updated (`~/.claude/projects/C--Users-Joshu-dev-ai-os/memory/`).
   - Unresolved questions — things you asked Joshua that never got a clear answer, or things he asked that got deflected.
3. **Do NOT audit the filesystem.** This is synthesis of what happened in THIS session. No `git log`, no broad `Glob` sweeps. If you didn't touch it this session, it doesn't belong here.
4. **Produce the output in chat.** Do not write a file. Do not update memory. Chat-only.

## Output template — use exactly this structure, every time

```
# Session Handoff — <one-line title of what this session was about>

## Where it started
<2-3 sentences: what Joshua asked for, key framing or constraints that emerged>

## Decisions locked + what shipped
- <decision or change> — <why, and where it lives (absolute path if a file)>
- ...

## Key files for next session
- `<absolute path>` — <why the next agent should read this first>
- Plan file: `<path>` (if a plan drove the session)
- Memory files touched: `<paths>` (if any)

## Running state
- Background processes: <shell IDs + what they are + how to kill> — or "none"
- Dev servers / ports: <url + port> — or "none"
- Open worktrees / branches: <paths> — or "none"

## Verified state as of <HH:MM>
- <claim about live/system state> — <how it was checked, and when>
- <claim> — NOT re-verified this session; last confirmed <when>
- ...

## Verification — how to confirm things still work
- `<command>` — <expected outcome>
- ...

## Deferred + open questions
- Deferred: <item> — <why pushed to later>
- OPEN (must be resolved or re-asked before new work): <question needing Joshua's input> — <context>

## Pick up here
<1-2 sentences: the single most likely next action for a fresh agent>
```

## Hard rules

1. **Chat output only.** Never write the handoff to a file. Never update memory from this skill. (That is `wrapup`'s job, not this one.)
2. **Never invent state.** If a section has nothing to report, write "none" — do not omit the section. Structure stability is the whole point.
3. **Absolute paths always.** The next agent may have a different working directory, or be Codex.
4. **If a plan file drove the session, name it first** in "Key files" so the next agent reads it before anything else.
5. **No emojis, no hype, no "great job" summaries.** Terse and concrete — paths, commands, shell IDs, decisions. Tone of a seasoned engineer handing off at end-of-shift.
6. **Background process IDs are critical.** If you started any `run_in_background` shells, their IDs must appear in "Running state" with the kill command — the next agent cannot find them otherwise.
7. **Stamp verified state, and separate it from assumed state.** Run `date "+%H:%M"` and use the real time. Every claim about live systems (deploy status, DB contents, what was sent, what is published) is either something you actually checked this session — say how — or explicitly marked NOT re-verified. A handoff that presents stale belief as current state makes the next session re-derive everything, which is the exact cost this skill exists to remove.
8. **Open questions are obligations, not notes.** Anything under OPEN must be resolved by the next session or re-asked to Joshua before it starts new work. Requirements have been silently re-typed across sessions, and a promised artifact once never arrived because nobody re-asked. If a question was open in the handoff you are picking up from, it is the first thing you raise.

## Anti-patterns — do not do these

- Summarizing the last 3 turns and calling it a handoff.
- Listing files by relative path.
- Skipping "Running state" because "nothing is running" — write "none" instead.
- Writing the summary to any file. This is chat-only by design.
- Adding a "what went well / what went poorly" retrospective. This isn't a retro.
- Recommending next steps beyond the single "Pick up here" line. The next agent decides; you just hand off.
